Nothing reclaimed what those left: GC and quota accounting walk `objects/` only, import recovery matches `{txn}`-prefixed names only, and both staging sweeps skipped dot-prefixed entries by contract. The bytes stayed invisible to every accounting path while counting in full against the free-space reserve, so a few kills ended with required captures failing `snapshot_quota_exceeded` and no operator-reachable way to reclaim. Both sweeps now unlink dot-prefixed temps older than `STAGING_TEMP_TTL_SECONDS` (24 h, longer than any single put, so an in-flight transfer is never mistaken for debris). The constant is public because two modules enforce the same rule and must not drift; the three comment blocks that documented the old *nothing reclaims these* contract are corrected. The reconcile-side sweep runs only on the archive-import path; recovery's runs on every session load, which is the one that matters.
  - **A kernel whose restore failed could never be persisted again (`backend/utils/sandbox.py`)**: `_restart_kernel` deliberately keeps a kernel alive after a failed best-effort `_restore_session()` — a corrupt dump must not brick a live kernel — but the same failure latches `_session_restore_failed`, and only a *successful* restore resets it. Restore runs at start and restart only, so every later `save_session()` returned immediately. The analysis carried on, recomputed its state from the uploaded data, and none of it reached `<sid>_session.pkl`; the next resume rehydrated the pre-crash dump instead. The guard's reasoning is right at the moment of failure and wrong afterwards, so it now expires rather than latching: the mark is `runtime_revision`, which advances only on a successful user cell, and a revision above the mark taken when the latch was set is a namespace this kernel rebuilt rather than restore residue. The latch is cleared only once the write has landed — clearing it at the guard, as the first version did, would have disarmed the protection permanently the first time a save raised, and left a stale watermark behind. Setting `_kernel_dead` instead was rejected: it contradicts `test_restore_failure_keeps_kernel_alive` and the stated design.
  - **One backed AnnData aborted the whole session save (`backend/utils/sandbox.py`)**: the save template promotes every AnnData in the namespace into the fail-closed required tier. A `sc.read_h5ad(path, backed='r')` wraps an open h5py handle dill refuses, so a read-only inspection of a large matrix cost the entire save. Backed objects are now dropped from `_required_names` and from the namespace and reported through the existing `__SCAI_SAVE_SKIPPED__` channel; their matrix is already on disk in the .h5ad, and the host-side required check is computed before it could know the object was backed, which is why both had to be pruned rather than one.
  - **A superseded report run re-dumped the runtime it had just been told to abandon (`backend/workflow/core.py`)**: `_superseded_report_result` cleared the session-scoped flag before the report runner's `finally: await workflow.close()`, which reads that flag *and* `workflow._restore_superseded` to decide `save=not superseded`. A report workflow is never registered in `state.workflows`, so `_retire_superseded_workflow` cannot reach it to set the second one, and `close()` computed `save=True`. The workflow-scoped flag is now set before the session-scoped one is cleared.
  - **The statistical gates refused work they had no evidence against (`backend/services/statistical_gates.py`, `backend/agents/bioinformatics_agent.py`, `backend/models/schema_parts/analysis.py`, `backend/agents/bioinformatics/tool_schemas.py`)**: three independent false-refusal paths, each burning agent steps and then stamping a false statistical-integrity warning into the round result and the final report, where it is indistinguishable from a real one. *An unset default read as a declared design*: `TreatmentGroup.sample_count` defaults to `1`, so a user who never edited the replicate count was reported as running a perfectly confounded 1-vs-1 experiment — and the check needs no between-condition claim to fire, only the substring `fdr` or `significant` anywhere in the findings text. A count of `1` is now believed only when at least one `DataFile` in that group carries an explicit `sample_number`; counts above 1 can only come from an edit and are always believed. This restores the principle the module's own tests already name: absent information must not be read as a violation. A genuine 1-vs-1 design is still refused, which is what the check is for. *An unsatisfiable requirement*: `check_ora_background` read `background_source`, a field `ScoreDefinition` did not declare and `register_score` had no property for, so it was always empty and the refusal told the agent to record the universe in exactly that unreachable field. The field now exists in the model, in the tool schema and in the handler, and the refusal names the argument rather than a bare field name. *A session-cumulative scan*: the gates iterated all of `state.score_definitions`, so one non-compliant score registered in round 2 re-refused every later round's unrelated findings, with no de-register tool to clear it. The scan is scoped to the current round (`round_number == 0` kept for restored state); `scoring_plans` stays unfiltered, because the gate uses it only as a `plan_id` lookup and a current-round score may legitimately cite a plan frozen earlier.
  - **A portable archive carried the endpoints the same change set had just started stripping (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/tar_builders.py`)**: `logs/llm.jsonl` was newly packed with no redaction, and every record carries `base_url` beside the prompt and response text. `strip_analysis_state_secrets` was applied to the `session.json` member only, so the archive withheld the endpoint in one place and shipped it in another — including on the `include_runtime=false` path the UI recommends for sharing. JSONL members are now rewritten on the way in with `ENDPOINT_FIELD_NAMES` blanked, keeping the real mtime and passing an unparseable line through verbatim so a torn final write cannot turn into an empty member. Other log files are unchanged and the per-agent usage numbers the detailed report reads are untouched.
  - **Export followed symlinks out of the session it was packing (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/tar_builders.py`)**: `_safe_add_file` builds each member from an already-open descriptor — which is what turns links into ordinary members and stops one stray `os.symlink` making an archive un-importable — and `_add_tree_to_tar` selects members with `is_file()`, which follows links. A link left in a workspace was therefore packed with its *target's* bytes. This is not an escalation: sandbox reads are ungated and same-uid, so a session owner could already copy another session's files without a symlink. It is silent inclusion of out-of-tree content in a session-scoped archive, and the last packer in the codebase that did not fail closed on an escaping link, which `services/snapshot_v2/workspace.py` has always done. Both tree walkers now drop them — the log walker included, which would otherwise have parsed and re-emitted another session's `llm.jsonl` as this session's own.
  - **A rejected download destroyed the archive it rejected (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/export_routes.py`)**: `export_download` popped the one-shot token before checking ownership, so any failure after that point stranded a multi-GB prepared export until the six-hour TTL sweep, and the retry got `404 Export not prepared`. The comment above the pop already applied this reasoning to the session binding; it now applies to the whole check. The token is peeked, binding, existence and ownership are checked, and consumption is last — a racing second redemption finds it gone and 404s, so the one-shot guarantee is unchanged. The reachable case is not the browser download, which is auth-optional and skips the ownership check entirely, but a leaked token redeemed by a signed-in non-owner.
  - **`collection` was the one unvalidated identifier in the gene-set path (`backend/services/gene_set_resolver.py`, `gene_set_fetch.py`)**: `set_id` is guarded by `validate_set_id`; its sibling `collection` went straight from the LLM tool call into a cache filename and the MSigDB release URL. The exploitable surface is narrow — the filename tail is always `.v<release>.<Hs|Mm>.symbols.gmt`, and a write additionally requires bytes fetched from the pinned host that parse as a GMT — so this closes an asymmetry rather than an arbitrary read or write. `validate_collection` rejects `..` and anything outside `[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._-]*`, enforced at the `load_collection` choke point every caller passes through. `version` remains unvalidated on the same paths and is the obvious next one.
  - **A direction was planned against another direction's questions (`backend/utils/direction_payload.py`)**: `resolve_direction_context`'s `previous_round` fallback ignored its `direction` argument and returned the first non-empty context it found walking backwards. Carrying a *predecessor* round's asks forward is deliberate, because a later direction is often generated from the previous round's next steps — but a *sibling* of the same batch was never asked to resolve this direction's questions. Its next steps were rendered into the planning prompt as open questions every step must address, and then reported to the user as coverage gaps in a plan that never owed them. Results attributed to a sibling of `active_direction_batch` are now skipped; the serial carry-forward is untouched, and with no active direction to be a sibling of, nothing is filtered at all — an empty key would otherwise have discarded the whole batch.
  - **The supplementary pass fired on rounds that belong to no batch (`backend/workflow/post_execution.py`)**: `_is_last_direction_round_of_batch` returned True whenever `current_round_direction` is empty, which is the exploration round and the legacy next-steps path — and since `planning_stage` is part of the idempotency id, round 1 ran it twice. It now returns False when there is no active direction; signals 1–3 still cover those rounds.
  - **What the review got wrong**: four of the fifteen findings did not survive verification, and recording why is worth more than the four fixes. The report-generation overwrite could not happen — a report workflow's sandbox is never started, so `save_session` would lazily start a kernel that rehydrates from the *post*-restore pkl; the residual cost was a spurious kernel start and a re-dump leaving the pkl no longer matching the manifest `_verify_live_restore` had just hashed. The backed-AnnData abort was misattributed to filter ordering: `_scai_is_volatile` never matches an AnnData at all, `_scai_stream_safe` already listed anndata so a non-required one aborted too, canonical `adata` aborted before this change as well, and Snapshot V2 capture does not go through `save_session`, so no HITL gate was ever involved — the real regression was confined to non-canonical names. The gene-set path was reported as arbitrary file read and write and is neither. And `_is_last_direction_round_of_batch` was reported as firing on every round of a single-direction batch, which is exactly once per batch as documented, because such a batch is one round. In the other direction two severities moved: the `recovery_blocked` clear is worse than reported because gate-off is the default, and the symlink dereference is milder because it grants nothing the sandbox did not already allow.
  - **No new tests**: this release adds none, which is a gap the convention of this section makes visible. Roughly fifteen behaviour changes ship unpinned, several on the default gate-off path. The existing suite was used as a regression harness rather than extended: the full backend run is `3931 passed, 79 skipped` with one failure, `test_citation_integrity.py::test_lfc_shrink_is_documented_with_its_required_argument`, which fails identically on an unmodified `22bc0c8` worktree built for the comparison — HEAD is not green, so a raw failure list proves nothing without that baseline. Two tests that pinned old behaviour did earn their keep, redirecting the quiescence and kernel-restart fixes toward designs that keep them green.

- `0.37.3` — A memory preflight refused `sc.pp.scale(adata, max_value=10)` on a 19,128 × 22,804 matrix and told the user it did so because "runtime snapshots" would be unsafe. Snapshot V2 was disabled in that deployment, and the host had 151 GiB free. Neither half of the refusal was true, and neither could be fixed by tuning it: the check read arbitrary user code statically, so it could not know a dtype, and it never consulted the feature it claimed to protect. The guard is deleted and the question moved to where it is answerable — the capture path, which knows exactly which object it is about to copy and how large that object actually is (2026-07-30):
  - **The static guard is gone (`backend/agents/bioinformatics/kernel_templates.py`, 10 re-export sites, `backend/tests/test_bioinformatics_runtime_guards.py`)**: `_build_resource_guard_preamble()`, `_high_memory_referenced_names()` and `_HIGH_MEMORY_ANALYSIS_RE` are removed along with their two injection points in the exec and figure wrappers. Three defects made tuning pointless. *Coverage*: the pattern list knew four operations (`scvelo` moments/velocity, `cellrank`, `sc.pp.scale`, `.X.toarray()`) and so missed `adata.to_df()`, `np.asarray(sparse)`, `adata.raw = adata.copy()` and every other route to the same allocation — and a miss ends in `SIGKILL`, which is worse than the false positive it was trading against. *Precision*: the estimate was `n_obs × n_vars × 8`, but scanpy's `.X` is `float32`; the refusal reported 3.2 GiB for 1.6 GiB of real allocation, which is what pushed it over the hardcoded 1.5 GiB ceiling — a constant that consulted no actual memory at all, while the adaptive headroom branch sat unreachable below it. *Attribution*: with several AnnData in one cell the guard sized `max()` of the candidates, so an already-subset `sc.pp.scale(adata_hvg, zero_center=False)` was still measured against the full `adata` whenever the cell mentioned it — and the `dense_transform` flag came from a regex that, unlike the AST pass beside it, could not see `zero_center=False`. The misleading traceback goes with it: the `MemoryError` was raised inside the wrapper's `try`, but the frame's line number had advanced through `finally`, so IPython pointed at `_scai_cleanup_plt.close('all')` — a line inside `except Exception: pass`, which could not have raised the exception being displayed.
  - **The capture measures what the capture is about to allocate (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/runtime_parts/kernel_templates.py`, `capture.py`)**: `_SAVE_ANNDATA_TEMPLATE` deep-copies each AnnData before sanitizing it, so the cost is not a prediction about someone else's code but a property of the next statement this process runs. `_INTROSPECT_CODE` — already executed once per capture — now also returns each AnnData's true byte size, the kernel's own `VmRSS`, and the tighter of `/proc/meminfo` `MemAvailable` and the cgroup remainder, at no extra round-trip. The byte walk covers `X`, `layers`, `obsp`, `varp`, `obsm`, `varm` and `.raw`, sums sparse `data`/`indices`/`indptr` rather than the container's meaningless `nbytes`, and de-duplicates by `id()` so a layer aliasing `X` is counted once; the probe pops its own helpers out of `globals()` afterwards. `_preflight_capture_memory()` refuses when the largest object's copy exceeds `SCAI_SNAPSHOT_MEMORY_SAFETY_RATIO` (default `0.8`, validated to `(0, 1]`) of that headroom. The bound is the largest single object, not the sum, because `_capture_adata` runs once per name and only one copy is ever live. Missing evidence never refuses: an unreadable probe, unknown headroom, an unmeasured target or an empty h5ad set all fall through to capturing.
  - **Nothing outside the capture path is checked**: with Snapshot V2 disabled the probe never runs and no memory is measured anywhere — sizing the host is the operator's job, and the container memory limit is the backstop. Ordinary analysis code is not policed even when snapshots are on. This is the scoping the deleted guard got wrong in both directions at once.
  - **A memory gate offers Proceed, not Retry (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/errors.py`, `failure_gate.py`, `backend/routes/lifecycle.py`, `backend/models/schemas.py`)**: `SNAPSHOT_FAILURE_ACTIONS_MEMORY` is `("proceed", "skip", "terminate")`. Retry is absent because the preflight is deterministic — it would measure the same objects against the same headroom and refuse again, and the gate could never clear, which is the same trap the old guard set for the agent. Manage is absent because it reclaims disk, not memory. `_actions_for_code()` applies this to all seven `allowed_actions` assignment sites (each of which overwrites the payload, so patching the entry point alone would not have held) and preserves the existing rule that `pre_restore` is never skippable: a memory refusal there yields `("proceed", "terminate")`. `POST /session/{id}/snapshot-failure/proceed` arms a one-shot `AnalysisState.force_snapshot_memory_override`, then delegates to the retry handler verbatim rather than cloning its producer dispatch; the capture consumes and clears the flag, so a later capture is judged on its own measurements.
  - **The frontend was silently dropping the new action (`frontend/src/utils/snapshotV2.ts`)**: `validSnapshotFailureActions()` is a hand-written type predicate, so an unrecognized name is filtered out with no error and no type failure — the backend would have sent `proceed`, the dialog would have rendered Skip and Terminate, and nothing would have pointed at the parser. All three parse entry points (`stores/store.ts`, `hooks/api/useWebSocket.ts`, `utils/transformLoadResponse.ts`) share the function, so one fix covers every path; `frontend/src/utils/__tests__/snapshotFailureActions.test.ts` now pins each action. `transformLoadResponse.ts` also stopped restating the action union as a literal type and imports `SnapshotFailureAction` instead.
  - **Memory sizing is documented rather than left to be discovered (`docs/admin-manual.md`, `docs/admin-manual-zh.md`)**: section 2 gains a sizing model fitted to a measured deployment — 2 samples / 19,128 cells / 22,804 genes reaching 6.4 GiB of kernel RSS — as `0.4 + cells × 330 KB + samples × 50 MB`, a table from 10,000 to 500,000 cells carrying 30% margin, and per-concurrent-analysis figures for snapshots on and off. The 200,000- and 500,000-cell rows are labelled extrapolation, since the fit has one measured point. Section 8 explains why a capture needs roughly 35% of the kernel's resident memory on top of what it already holds (the h5ad copy plus the dill readback, which deserializes a second copy to verify the round-trip), and documents the gate's three choices. Both manuals now tell operators to run with `--memory`: the deployment that hit this had no container limit, so a runaway kernel could have taken the host's other services with it.
  - **New tests**: `test_snapshot_v2_memory_preflight.py` (20 cases: the reference deployment passing, refusal below headroom, largest-not-sum, safety-ratio sensitivity, six missing-evidence paths, payload parsing, and the action sets for skippable and non-skippable producers) and `frontend/src/utils/__tests__/snapshotFailureActions.test.ts` (5). Extended: the failure-dialog suite for the memory gate's buttons and copy. `force_snapshot_memory_override` is registered in `test_to_api_dict_coverage.py`'s exclusions — it is armed and consumed server-side and the client never reads it. Backend 3931 pass / 79 skip, frontend 252 pass; the single remaining backend failure is `test_citation_integrity::test_lfc_shrink_is_documented_with_its_required_argument`, which is `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'pydeseq2'` on the host interpreter and fails identically on the unmodified tree.

- `0.37.2` — The offline marker priors were running on 9% of PanglaoDB, and had been since the projection was fixed. The fix landed in `0.36.8-rc1`; the table did not, because a downloaded table lived in `backend/data/markers/` where `COPY . .` baked it into the image, and availability was decided by `Path.exists()` — so nothing re-downloads a file that is already there. Every deployment kept serving a table generated on 2026-07-18 by the code that dropped dual-species rows. Making the cache a cache exposed the rest: the supplementary marker source had never once been fetched, the same location bug applies to the gene-set cache, and merging in the supplementary source turned out to make cluster annotation worse before it made it better (2026-07-30):
  - **The tables live on the data volume, not in the code tree (`backend/services/marker_priors.py`, `scripts/fetch_markers.py`, `.dockerignore`, `.gitignore`, `start.sh`, `backend/data/markers/` deleted)**: `runtime_markers_dir()` resolves `$SCAI_DATA_DIR/markers` and `default_dest_dir()` mirrors the same rule on the script side, which cannot import `backend` because it is loaded by path from both the CLI and `trigger_fetch`. That duplication is pinned by two tests rather than left to drift, since a divergence would have the backend read one directory while the fetch writes another and DB priors would be permanently unavailable with nothing logged. `.dockerignore` gains `backend/data/markers/` and `backend/data/gene_sets/` explicitly — its existing `data/` entry is per-directory and never matched `backend/data/` — and a test asserts no marker table exists anywhere under the repo. The licence text now travels with the data (`fetch_and_write` writes `LICENSE` beside the tables), because the directory that used to hold it is gone. `start.sh`'s prefetch drops its hardcoded file check: `fetch_and_write` returns early when the table is already current, so the launcher can call it unconditionally.
  - **A stale table reads as absent instead of as available (`marker_priors.MARKER_SCHEMA_REVISION`, `fetch_markers.SCHEMA_REVISION`)**: existence was never the right question, because the tables are a cache whose *correct contents* changed. Every table now carries `SCHEMA_REVISION` in its header; `MarkerDB.usable()` (replacing `present()`) is true only for the current revision, `_load()` skips and warns on anything else, and the two constants are asserted equal by a test — bumping only the writer would silently disable priors, because the reader would reject everything the writer produces. Revision 2 is the dual-species and `organ`-as-`tissue` projection; revision 3 adds the supplementary source. The bump is what forces already-downloaded deployments forward, and is the discipline the `0.36.8-rc1` fix was missing.
  - **CellMarker is reachable for the first time (`_download_cellmarker`, `CELLMARKER_URL`)**: the only endpoint was `https://117.50.127.228/...`, a bare IP whose TLS cannot be verified, so the code had to choose between pinning a SHA-256 and refusing to run — and shipping unpinned, it refused, every time, fail-secure and completely silent. CellMarker's own domain presents a valid chain, so `verify=True` is now unconditional and the whole pin/`verify=False` branch is deleted; a source-level test (comments stripped) guards against the escape hatch returning, and another asserts both source URLs are `https` named hosts. Upstream is CellMarker 3.0, which ships zip-wrapped TSV rather than `.xlsx` and renamed `Symbol` to `symbol`; `single_cell_marker.zip` is the subset taken — 12 MB against 71 MB for the full table, whose uncompressed 811 MB adds only bulk and antibody-derived rows — read with `usecols` so a 148 MB TSV does not balloon the backend process. PanglaoDB is no longer handed to `pd.read_csv(URL)` either: pandas sends urllib's User-Agent and the host 403s UAs it reads as scrapers (`"we don't permit curl access anymore, b/c bots"`), so the bytes are fetched explicitly with a declared UA and a timeout.
  - **Merging the supplementary source degraded annotation before it improved it, measured on a real run's clusters (`backend/services/ora_kernel.py`, `backend/agents/pi/shared.py`)**: the candidate pool went from 178 cell types to 1071, and replaying a mouse-liver session's marker lists showed the answer being pushed out rather than refined. On cluster 0 — unambiguous hepatocytes — `Brush cell`, `Early enterocyte precursor cell` and `Goblet cell` each fired on the same two Cyp genes at the same p-value, filled all of `top_k=3`, and `Hepatocytes` never reached the prompt. `_collapse_synonymous` merges hits sharing an identical overlap into one slot that names the alternatives, and `_prior_label` renders them; the correct coarse label returns to first. A first attempt at a tissue label made things worse and was replaced: listing an arbitrary three of a cell type's organs reported `CD8 T cell` as `Kidney`, so `_tissue_relevance` now answers only whether the cell type is annotated in the tissue under analysis, plus how many organs it spans. It is reported, never used to filter — organ names are free text on both sides and a miss must not be able to drop a correct hit — and in the ranking it breaks ties only, never overriding a smaller p-value, which two tests pin from both directions. `_compute_database_priors` gained the `tissue` argument that feeds it.
  - **The dedup was discarding all but one organ per marker (`marker_priors._join_tissues`, `TISSUE_SEP`)**: aggregating `tissue` with `"first"` was invisible while nothing read the column, and wrong the moment the tissue check did — CellMarker annotates one marker across many tissues, so cell types genuinely present in the analysed organ answered "not in this tissue". Organs are now unioned into a `;`-joined field that the kernel splits, and `str()` sentinels (`'nan'`, `'None'`) are filtered rather than treated as organ names.
  - **The gene-set cache had the same location bug (`backend/services/gene_set_resolver.py`, `gene_set_fetch.py`)**: `_DEFAULT_CACHE` pointed at `backend/data/gene_sets`, so a downloaded MSigDB GMT was equally capturable by an image build. Replaced by a lazy `runtime_gene_sets_dir()` reading `$SCAI_DATA_DIR/gene_sets`; `load_collection` is the only caller of the default and is covered by a test asserting the `cache_dir=None` path lands on the data volume and creates it.
  - **Verified against the real sources, not fixtures**: a fetch into a scratch data directory produced PanglaoDB at 15760 rows (7909 mouse / 7851 human), 178 cell types, tissue populated on every row — against 746 rows, 121 cell types and an empty tissue column before — and CellMarker at 225929 rows across 1683 cell types. Replaying the mouse-liver clusters end to end through `MarkerDB` → `write_workspace_input` → `compute_ora_priors` → the prompt block puts `Hepatocytes` and `Macrophage` first with `tissue: matches`, where the Kupffer-cell cluster previously received no prior at all and cluster 11 sharpened from `T memory cells` (3 overlapping genes) to `CD8 T cell` (15). 3917 backend tests pass, 79 skip, 2 fail — `test_secret_persistence.py::test_langgraph_checkpoint_serializer_never_contains_raw_key` and `test_snapshot_v2_feature_gate.py::test_all_snapshot_v2_http_operations_use_central_dependency`, both of which fail identically on the unmodified `0.37.1` tree.

- `0.37.1` — The `geneset-optimize` line merged into main. Two development lines had run in parallel from `0.36.8`: gene-set authority and post-execution auditing on one side, session export/import and Snapshot V2 hardening on the other. They touch disjoint subsystems, so the merge is a union rather than a reconciliation — the only textual conflicts were the version string and this changelog (2026-07-30):
  - **Gene-set and methodology work arrives as released in `0.36.8-rc1`..`rc7`**: gene-set provenance and authority, scoring-plan pre-registration, cross-organism gene-set fetch, the methodological rule corpus, statistical validity gates, direction-payload propagation, plan-revision content preservation, and the post-execution plan-vs-evidence audit with its gated supplementary pass. The individual entries below are kept as written rather than collapsed, because each documents a decision the merged code still carries.
  - **The co-edited sources merged as written**: `backend/workflow/core.py`, `planning.py`, `synthesis.py`, `agents.py`, `backend/models/schemas.py`, `backend/config/_config.py`, `backend/agents/bioinformatics_agent.py`, `frontend/src/components/AnalysisPanel.tsx` and both i18n locale files were edited on both sides and merged without conflict; each side's change lands in a different region of the file, and the union is what the suite below was run against.
  - **One clean auto-merge was wrong and was overridden (`backend/tests/test_snapshot_v2_runtime_edges.py`)**: the branch's only change to this file was accidental — `0.36.8-rc1`, `rc4` and `rc5` each re-appended the merge-base's section 15 (`test_terminate_clears_a_pending_skip_marker`, `test_run_clears_a_stale_skip_marker`), five stale copies and not one new assertion. Appending to the end of a file conflicts with nothing, so git took it; and because a later `def` shadows an earlier one, the stale copies silently replaced the version `0.36.10` had fixed for the `has_resumable_checkpoint` branch in `routes/lifecycle.py`, reintroducing `TypeError: '>' not supported between instances of 'MagicMock' and 'int'` in a merge that reported no conflict. The file takes main's content unchanged. A duplicate-definition scan over every `.py` touched by either side found no other shadowed name.
  - **Verified on the merge result, not on either parent**: 3881 backend tests pass, 79 skip, and 245 frontend tests across 38 files pass with `tsc --noEmit` clean. One backend test fails — `test_citation_integrity.py::test_lfc_shrink_is_documented_with_its_required_argument`, which imports `pydeseq2`; it fails identically on the branch tip, because the package is declared in `requirements.txt` but is not installed in this environment. The merge neither caused it nor fixes it.
  - **Version**: the merge is released as `0.37.1`; `0.36.8-rc7` was a pre-release of a branch that never shipped on its own, so no `0.36.8` patch line is retained.

**0.36.x — (2026-07-25 to 2026-07-30)**

- `0.36.14` — Session export/import in the configuration everything actually ships in. Snapshot V2 defaults to off and no launcher sets the gate, while the test suite forced it on — so the shipped default was the one configuration nothing could run: 35 assertions failed under it and one integration test deadlocked the whole pytest process. Making that testable exposed the rest: a workflow checkpoint could resume a graph against a kernel that never arrived, a live runtime could travel inside the export that declares the runtime absent, an imported HITL gate could offer a dialog whose every button answers 404 forever, and every member the importer silently dropped was reported to the user as "Import successful" (2026-07-30):
  - **The shipped default is testable, and green (`conftest.py`, `backend/tests/integration/test_snapshot_v2_generation_consistency.py`, twelve `test_snapshot_*.py` / `test_chat_snapshot_safety.py` / `test_auto_snapshot_terminate.py` files, `test_snapshot_v1_cutover.py`)**: the root conftest sets `SCAI_ENABLE_SNAPSHOT_V2` with `setdefault`, so the suite inherited whatever the environment said and could only ever be forced *on*. Under the operator default it reported 35 failures — every one a Snapshot V2 tripwire firing because a capture correctly did not happen — plus `test_auto_snapshot_waits_for_pending_save`, which hung forever rather than failing: `_create_auto_snapshot` returns early when disabled, so the test's `asyncio.Event` never opened, its `wait_for` raised before the line that releases the gate, and the still-installed monkeypatch stranded the fixture's own `sandbox.stop()` teardown. `--timeout`, `--timeout-method=thread` and `faulthandler_timeout` all failed to fire, because the hang is inside fixture finalization. A new `requires_snapshot_v2` marker plus the autouse `_pin_snapshot_v2_gate` fixture pins the gate on for tests that certify the enabled lifecycle — patching `_config.SNAPSHOT_V2_ENABLED` rather than the environment, since the flag is a module global read at call time — so those files behave identically with the variable set or unset, while tests asserting *disabled* behaviour keep patching it off themselves and deliberately carry no marker. The deadlocking test also releases its gate in a `finally`, so a future early return fails one test instead of the suite. `test_snapshot_v1_restore_cutover_defaults_on` asserted `SystemConfig().snapshot_v2_enabled is True`, which contradicts the documented default and only held because of the conftest; since that field's `Field(default=...)` is bound at class-definition time and no monkeypatch can reach it, the assertion now pins the mirror (`is _config._ENABLE_SNAPSHOT_V2`) — the invariant that matters, V1 restore staying rejected whatever the flag says, is pinned by the test below it.
  - **A checkpoint is kept only when the kernel it describes arrives with it (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/import_impl.py`)**: the LangGraph checkpoint was dropped on an id reallocation only, so a runtime export imported onto a *free* id kept it while the pkl — which additionally needs `X-Trust` — did not. `resume()` calls `aget_state()` before it ever consults `restore_start_intent`, and that intent is read only in its "no checkpoint found" tail, so the arming that `_arm_restore_intent_after_import` and `POST /run` both perform was bypassed: the run replayed the source machine's graph node against an empty kernel, and `POST /run` had already cleared the `runtime_restore_status` block that would otherwise have stopped it. `states/<id>_bioinfo_checkpoint.json` was worse, because it matches neither `is_sandbox_pickle` nor `_is_checkpoint_member` and therefore travelled in the *default* export: `has_resumable_checkpoint` then made the bioinformatics node restore the old conversation and discard the freshly built instruction, including the kernel-is-empty notice `runtime_recompute_pending` exists to deliver. New `_drop_kernelless_checkpoints` removes both families when no live runtime landed, which is only decidable after the member walk — member order in a tar is not guaranteed — and must run before `_arm_restore_intent_after_import` tests for those files. The `report` checkpoint family is deliberately untouched: report generation resumes from the round results in the state, not from anything in the kernel. Exact continuation now requires all three of `include_runtime=true`, `X-Trust: true`, and the archive's session id still free on the destination; the two tests that pinned the old behaviour did so without arguing for it (one had no docstring and an archive with no pkl at all) and were rewritten around the kernel condition.
  - **The h5ad runtime sidecars are runtime (`export_import_parts/tar_builders.py`, `import_impl.py`)**: `runtime_h5ad_manifest.json` and the `states/*.h5ad` files it names are written by a Snapshot V2 restore so a kernel can come back holding `adata` when the dill residual cannot carry it — and matching neither export filter, the pair shipped in the portable export that also marks the runtime `unavailable`, then landed with no trust assertion at all. The sandbox rehydrates from the manifest alone, so the imported session came up holding an `adata` frozen at the exporting installation's last restore while its own state said the runtime was gone and `/run` told the agent the kernel was empty. Export now filters the pair under `include_runtime` and import gates it behind `X-Trust`, which is what that header already documents ("restore runtime objects (dill/h5ad)"). Both halves move together or not at all: a manifest whose sidecars are missing makes every later kernel start raise `h5ad sidecar missing`, which is a brick rather than a degrade, and orphan sidecars are inert because nothing reads `states/*.h5ad` except through the manifest. Availability detection now credits a manifest *this* import wrote, since a "complete" restore target with no dill residual leaves no pkl at all and the manifest is then the only carrier of `adata` — without that, the fix would have sent a genuine migration through a re-run it does not need and dropped the checkpoints that still match it.
  - **Import no longer hands over a gate only a live workflow could release (`import_impl.py`, `backend/services/session_store.py`)**: `_normalize_imported_status` rewrote `RUNNING`/`GENERATING_REPORT` to `PAUSED` but left `WAITING_INPUT` untouched, and `POST /{id}/llm-failure-action` — the only exit for `llm_failure`, `agent_disabled` and `marker_db_missing` — resolves them through `workflows[session_id]`, which no import creates. The WebSocket parks such a session in its HITL-hold branch and returns *before* registering a workflow, after re-opening the dialog, so the user got Retry/Skip/Modify buttons that raise `404 Workflow not found` forever. `ensure_session_loaded` cleared two of the three on a disk load, but an import primes the cache, so that path was never reached — and `agent_disabled` was missing from it anyway, making it the one gate that never self-healed even across a restart. Both are fixed: the import clears any workflow-bound gate (keyed on the reason, not the status, so a stale pairing cannot smuggle it through) and the disk-load cleanup gains `agent_disabled` plus `agent_disabled_info`, which otherwise kept riding out on every `state_sync` as an open dialog. Gates with their own resume route, such as `species_input`, are deliberately left standing.
  - **Import normalizes the state it writes, not just the state it returns (`import_impl.py`, `import_routes.py`)**: nothing on any import path called `normalize_disabled_snapshot_state`, so a session from an installation that had Snapshot V2 enabled arrived carrying every V2-only blocker — `snapshot_failure`, `pending_required_snapshot`, `skip_required_snapshot_phase`, `wait_input_reason`, `recovery_blocked`, `snapshot_transaction.recovery_error`. With the gate off the first authenticated read repaired them, but only there; with it on no load path clears them at all, so `/run` answered 409 and `/resume` refused, pointing the user at snapshot actions whose objects an ordinary archive never carried. A new `_finalize_imported_state` runs the three existing fixups in the one correct order — the normalizer before the status normalizer, because `_stable_status` passes `generating_report` straight through and that is exactly the status the frontend renders no controls for — and is now the single call site for all three import paths. The crash-safety write also carried the *pre*-normalization payload, so a process that died before its own save left the gate on disk; it now writes the normalized state in externalized form, byte-for-byte what `async_save_session` writes moments later. Both corrective saves are forced: `PAUSED` does not bypass the save throttle, and for the inline `.json`/`.zip` paths that write is the only `session.json` the session ever gets.
  - **An explicit start intent is not answered with a report (`backend/routes/websockets.py`)**: on a session imported at its final round, `POST /run` writes `RUNNING`, which was enough to satisfy the report-only predicate — a predicate scoped by its own comment to "sessions that completed analysis but were interrupted during report generation", not to a user-initiated run. So Re-run generated a final report out of the imported rounds, `should_resume_workflow` was never consulted, and because `/run` had already cleared the runtime warning the finished session's only remaining trace of an empty kernel was `runtime_recompute_pending`, which only the bioinformatics node can consume. The decision is lifted to `_needs_report_only()` and an armed `restore_start_intent` now suppresses it, while `final_report_requested` keeps precedence. Suppression is sufficient on its own: `_should_continue` tests `current_round >= max_rounds` only *after* a round body runs, so the preserved round re-executes and then routes to the report exactly once — raising `max_rounds` instead would have run an extra round, since `preserve_round_on_initialize` keeps the round where it is.
  - **A failed kernel restore is no longer written over the good pkl (`backend/utils/sandbox.py`, `sandbox_parts/kernel.py`)**: `_restore_session` assigns into `globals()` frame by frame, so an aborted restore leaves a partial namespace — and it raises, correctly, rather than degrading. But the raise left `km`/`kc` alive holding that namespace, nothing downstream re-checks (`execute()` guards only on `kc is None`, the agent only on `km is None`), and the next thing the chat path does is `stop()` with `save=True`. `save_session` had no failed-restore guard, so the truncated namespace was dumped over the live pkl and re-signed: the only good copy destroyed, and the *next* start "succeeding" with a silently truncated runtime still reporting `runtime_restore_status` as complete. `save_session` now refuses outright when the flag is set, `start()` tears the kernel down with `save=False` before re-raising so the next start rebuilds clean, and the error text stops recommending a snapshot restore that the shipped configuration cannot offer.
  - **Import reports what it dropped (`import_impl.py`, `frontend/src/components/Sidebar.tsx`, `i18n/locales/{en,zh}.ts`)**: the worker knew exactly which members it skipped — V2 storage, unsafe paths, off-layout entries, untrusted pickles, and now untrusted h5ad sidecars and dropped checkpoints — and told only the log, under a modal that said "Import successful". Counters ride on the existing `outcome` channel, are mutated in place so a cancel or mid-archive failure still reads a partial summary, and reach the completed job record with zero-valued kinds filtered so the common case stays an empty dict; the post-walk checkpoint drop reports through a `notify` hook, since it is the one decision that cannot be counted inside the loop. The examples list is capped at 20 because an off-layout archive can hold tens of thousands of members and the list is echoed on every status poll. The wizard renders one human line in both languages and keeps `success: true` — a skipped member is not a failed import — and renders nothing extra when the archive came through whole.
  - **The archive states what it could not carry (`export_routes.py`, `export_import_parts/core.py`)**: `_build_session_tar` packs no Snapshot V2 storage root at all, in either gate mode, while `session.json` ships every `format_version >= 2` record — so a destination could not tell "the source had no snapshots" from "the export dropped them", and with the gate off those records can be neither restored (404/410) nor deleted (409). The manifest now carries `snapshot_v2_records_without_payload`, counted from the payload the packer reads one line earlier. Whether such payloads *should* travel in an ordinary archive, and whether a gate-off installation may delete a payload-less record, are left open as product decisions rather than guessed at.
  - **A refusal names the real constraint (`import_impl.py`)**: with the gate off, every member shape a V2 export emits is filtered before the session id is even latched, so the ordinary import walked the whole archive and ended at `400 "Session state JSON not found in archive"` — blaming the file for an operator setting. That case now says the archive carries only Snapshot V2 data and needs a server with the subsystem enabled, while the genuinely-malformed case keeps its original message byte-for-byte. The environment variable is deliberately absent from the detail: this text reaches end users, and the operator-facing wording already lives in `_config.py` and `main.py`.
  - **Export sizes the volume it writes to, and its temp files are reclaimable (`export_import_parts/core.py`, `export_routes.py`, `backend/tasks/session_cleanup.py`, `backend/main.py`)**: an export is a second full-size copy of the session written to the same data volume, and the 1 GiB free-space floor had exactly one consumer — the import side. `check_export_disk_space()` mirrors it on both export entry points, placed after ownership resolution so an unauthorized caller still gets a 404 rather than a 507 confirming the session exists, and before the save and the pack so a refusal writes nothing. Reclamation walked only the in-memory `prepared_exports` dict, so a restart forgot every token and stranded the files with nothing left that could name them; a filesystem sweep now runs at startup (unconditional, safe only there — the dict is empty by construction before any request is served) and periodically under the existing TTL, restricted to the three prefixes the export endpoints write. The streaming export's temp file gains a prefix so a leak from an aborted download is distinguishable from an import upload's temp file and therefore sweepable at all.
  - **A HEAD on the export route no longer packs the session (`export_routes.py`)**: the route is registered for GET *and* HEAD with no branch, so an authenticated owner's HEAD forced a session save and a full multi-GB build whose body starlette then discarded. It now short-circuits after ownership resolution — keeping GET's exact 400/401/404 surface so it cannot become an existence oracle — and returns the same headers without building anything. The route registration is untouched, so `route_snapshot.json` is unchanged.
  - **Three lies in the import wizard (`frontend/src/components/Sidebar.tsx`)**: the extension test was case-sensitive while the backend lowercases before dispatching, so `EXPORT.TAR.GZ` took the background branch server-side while the client skipped polling, deleted its pending-import marker and announced success over an extraction still in flight. Inline `.zip`/`.json` imports register no job, so the lost-response fallback polled an id that 404s and reported "Import failed" after ~30 s for an import that had succeeded; that path now says the outcome is unknown and where to look, which is the truth. And neither completion path synced the tab, so an imported mid-analysis session left the user on the Data tab with Resume and Re-run hidden behind `display:none` — unlike every other path in the file.
  - **Two contained residuals (`export_import_parts/core.py`, `import_routes.py`)**: `_is_disabled_snapshot_v2_member` keyed its session-root rule on `parts[1]` after only `strip("/")`, so `./<sid>/v2/…` and `<sid>//objects/…` walked past it — harmless only because the destination resolver's whitelist is what actually blocked them, which made this filter's own answer wrong rather than merely redundant; empty and `.` segments are now dropped before anything is compared. The `.json` import inlined blobs from the *archive's* session directory, which on an id collision belongs to a different live session; it now reads the destination, leaving the tokens dangling, which is the honest outcome for a document that shipped no sidecars.
  - **Documentation corrected against the code (`docs/admin-manual.md`, `docs/admin-manual-zh.md`, `docs/HANDOVER.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, `AGENTS.md`, `clients/python/scai_client/job.py`)**: the manual's one content guarantee — "excludes session logs and credentials in every case" — was half false: logs are packed unconditionally, and they have to be, since they are the only per-call record of model usage and a report regenerated without them claims every agent made zero calls. The runtime paragraph promised that a runtime export "resumes on a different installation as well as the original one" without the precondition that decides it, and now states all three requirements plus what a colliding id actually costs. The warning that import "can write over files for an existing session with that ID" was exactly inverted — import claims ids with `mkdir(exist_ok=False)` and never overwrites — and the warning that an import job id "is not scoped to its creator" was false in both endpoints, which answer an unknown id and another user's id identically with 404. `CLAUDE.md` and `AGENTS.md` cited `docs/verification/snapshot-v2-acceptance.md` as evidence for the alias residual; that file was added at 0.34.1 and deleted at 0.34.4, so both now point at HANDOVER section 8 and say plainly that no test asserts the residual and no operator acceptance run is recorded. The HANDOVER entry claiming a README Snapshot V2 section is annotated rather than rewritten, since this file is append-only history. The SDK's `download_export` docstring stopped calling an arbitrary workspace zip "the full session export".
  - **Three findings were refuted, not fixed**: export does *not* generally turn a running session into a completed one — that happens only when the failure's `retry_context.producer` is `post_report`; there is no window in which a session exported between Start and its first completed round claims a kernel it lacks, because `initialize_round` has already advanced `current_round`; and the obvious remedy for a bad imported pkl — degrade instead of raising — is unsafe for the reason the raise exists, so the raise stays and the damage it left behind was fixed instead.
  - **Left open as product decisions**: whether Snapshot V2 payloads should travel in an ordinary archive at all; whether a gate-off installation may delete a `format_version >= 2` record it can neither restore nor verify; whether `GET /api/v1/analyses/{id}/export` should keep its documented workspace-zip short-circuit, redact the session JSON it falls back to, and close its `mkstemp` descriptor; and whether a poisoned runtime should be quarantined automatically, which is what would make the bioinformatics agent's existing "fresh environment" fallback reachable for the first time.
  - **New tests (`backend/tests/test_export_import_hardening.py`, `test_secret_persistence.py`, `test_session_store.py`, `test_sandbox_failure_surfacing.py`, `test_integration_ws_guard.py`, `test_e2e_http_roundtrip.py`, `frontend/src/components/__tests__/Sidebar.import-outcome.test.tsx`)**: 23 backend cases and 6 frontend cases, each written red-before-green — the h5ad pair's atomicity in both directions, the trusted h5ad-only runtime, the kernel-less checkpoint drop against the report family that must survive, the three workflow-bound gates cleared at import and on load with `species_input` left standing, the V2 blockers gone from the returned state *and* from `session.json`, the same blockers preserved when the gate is on, the skip summary reaching the caller and staying empty for a whole archive, the V2-only refusal wording, `save_session` refusing after a failed restore, the teardown on that path, report-only firing for an interrupted report but not for an explicit start, and the wizard's upper-cased tar.gz, lost inline response, tab sync and skip line. The suite now runs in both gate modes: backend 3512 passed / 79 skipped with the gate on and the same with it off, frontend 242 passed.

- `0.36.13` — Snapshot V2 capacity and honest labelling: per-session quota raised to 500 GiB and the free-space reserve to 50 GiB, both made operator-tunable before startup; the panel advises a cleanup at 90% instead of letting the ceiling refuse a capture unannounced; and the subsystem is finally documented for developers and operators — as in development and not stable, still absent from the user guide, and marked experimental in the UI (2026-07-30):
  - **The two ceilings are operator-tunable (`backend/config/_config.py`, `docs/admin-manual.md`, `admin-manual-zh.md`)**: `snapshot_v2_session_quota_bytes` and `snapshot_v2_free_space_reserve_bytes` had defaults but no way to set them — no environment binding (unlike the `SCAI_ENABLE_SNAPSHOT_V2` gate beside them) and no route exposing them, so `/api/config` reported only the boolean and an operator wanting different capacity had to edit code. New `SCAI_SNAPSHOT_SESSION_QUOTA_GB` and `SCAI_SNAPSHOT_FREE_SPACE_RESERVE_GB` are read once at import through `_parse_capacity_gb_env`, in GiB because that is the unit someone sizing a data volume works in while every check downstream is in bytes. An unset, non-integer or non-positive value logs and falls back rather than raising: this is import-time, so refusing would turn a typo in a deployment environment into a backend that cannot start. Defaults move from 100 GiB / 5 GiB to **500 GiB / 50 GiB**.
  - **A quota below the advised floor is warned about, not clamped (`backend/config/_config.py`)**: `warn_if_quota_below_advisory` logs at startup when the per-session quota is under `SNAPSHOT_SESSION_QUOTA_ADVISORY_MINIMUM_GB` (100 GiB) and returns whether it fired. The operator's value still applies — a small volume is a legitimate reason — but the symptom of an undersized quota is not gradual: a single runtime capture of a large AnnData already runs to tens of GB, and the store retains every distinct object across a session's lineage rather than only the newest, so the capture that crosses the ceiling fails closed and parks the analysis on a snapshot-failure gate. It is a callable rather than a bare `if` at module scope so it can be tested without reloading the module: a reload rebinds `default_config`, and every consumer that captured it by reference at import time keeps the old object, which leaks a different ceiling into the rest of the test session.
  - **The two modules that cannot import config no longer drift from it (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/object_store.py`, `runtime_parts/proof.py`)**: `SnapshotImportLimits` takes a duck-typed config and `proof.py` holds `RUNTIME_CAPTURE_FREE_SPACE_RESERVE_BYTES` at module scope, so both carry a hand-copied default — and a `SystemConfig` change that forgot them would leave the import path and the runtime-capture path enforcing the old ceiling while everything else moved. Both mirrors are updated and `test_capacity_defaults_mirror_system_config` now fails if they diverge again.
  - **The listing endpoint reports usage against the ceiling that will refuse the next capture (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/management.py`, `service_parts/queries.py`)**: `SnapshotStoreStatus` gains `quota_bytes`, `quota_usage_ratio` and `quota_warning`, resolved server-side so the fraction a user is advised at and the ceiling `_check_quota` enforces cannot disagree. The ratio compares *physical* bytes, matching what the quota counts — logical bytes count every reference and would warn far too early on a lineage that dedups well — and is deliberately not clamped at 1.0, since a store can legitimately sit above the ceiling after an operator lowers the quota or via the pre_restore grace allowance, and reporting 1.0 there would hide how far over it is. A non-positive quota yields no warning rather than a 100%-full store: that is "no ceiling known here", and advice the user cannot act on. `store_status()` passes the service's own resolved quota, because the constructor accepts a per-instance override and a status measured against the global default would advise a cleanup at the wrong point.
  - **The panel advises a cleanup at 90% (`frontend/src/components/SnapshotPanel.tsx`, `frontend/src/types/snapshotV2.ts`)**: an amber `role="alert"` block above the storage summary, in both languages, showing the percentage and both figures. It renders on the backend's `quota_warning` flag and never re-derives a threshold from the ratio, so the number the user sees and the number the capture enforces stay one value owned in one place. The quota trio is typed optional: a response without it — an older backend, or a fixture predating the fields — renders nothing rather than "NaN%".
  - **Snapshots are documented, and documented as experimental (`docs/HANDOVER.md`, `docs/admin-manual.md`, `admin-manual-zh.md`)**: the subsystem had no prose anywhere — only scattered route-table rows and changelog entries — so an operator had no way to learn what it stores, where, what it costs, or that it is not finished. HANDOVER gains section 8 (storage model, lifecycle table, the safe-point handshake, continuation capsules, capacity, and a known-limits list including the object-alias-identity residual); both administrator manuals gain section 8 (how to enable it, where snapshots live, event-driven GC, the per-session quota's worst case, transactional restore and recovery-blocking, what to monitor). Every new section opens with a status block: in development, not stable, disabled by default, layout and API may change without migration, no operator acceptance run recorded, and not a backup mechanism. Inserting a section shifted the numbering in all three files — the one prose cross-reference that moved (`section 12` → `13` for portable sessions) was updated, and the new sections' own references were corrected to the post-shift numbers.
  - **The user guide still says nothing (`docs/user-guide.md`, `user-guide-cn.md`)**: deliberately untouched, and verified to contain zero mentions. The feature is not offered to end users while it is experimental, so documenting it there would advertise a guarantee that does not exist.
  - **The snapshot panel says it is experimental (`frontend/src/components/SnapshotPanel.tsx`)**: an amber `Experimental` badge beside the title, with a hover explanation, and a one-line caveat strip under the header stating that the format may change and older snapshots are not guaranteed to restore. Both are unconditional rather than flag-driven: the panel is only reachable on an instance whose operator set `SCAI_ENABLE_SNAPSHOT_V2`, so rendering at all means the feature is on. The caveat sits outside the loading branch so a slow listing cannot make it look like a settled feature. Three tests in `SnapshotQuotaWarning.test.tsx` pin the marking in both languages and before the listing resolves, so it cannot be dropped silently.
  - **New tests (`backend/tests/test_snapshot_v2_capacity.py`, `frontend/src/components/__tests__/SnapshotQuotaWarning.test.tsx`)**: 22 backend cases and 7 frontend cases covering the new defaults, the mirror guard, the parser's fallback behaviour on six unusable values, the advisory floor firing and not firing, the threshold's inclusive boundary at exactly 90%, an over-quota store, an unknown ceiling, physical-vs-logical comparison, a per-instance quota override, the panel's three render paths, and the experimental marking. Backend 3489 passed / 79 skipped, frontend 236 passed.

- `0.36.12` — Snapshot V2 edge-case hardening, in two audit passes over the whole subsystem (the second one auditing the first): the restore a user asks for at a HITL gate can be acknowledged at every one of the seven gates, quiescence is re-proved rather than assumed once, rollback is neither undone by the session cache nor half-reported, a crashed restore job or export stops protecting its target forever, object writes prove what they wrote and cannot leak their temp, the pre-write ceilings actually fire, pre_restore records a position that agrees with its own capsule, the discard-current escape hatch is both reachable and able to succeed, import/export atomicity and admission control, and the frontend effects that erased state or defeated their own bounds (2026-07-30):
  - **All seven HITL gates hand the safe point over (`backend/workflow/helpers.py`, `core.py`, `synthesis.py`, `annotation.py`, `planning.py`, `callbacks.py`)**: every HITL gate blocks *inside* a graph node, so the run task stays in flight and the graph never reaches another node entry — the only site that acknowledged the cooperative safe point. A restore requested while the user sat at a gate therefore had no acknowledger at all: `_acknowledge_if_quiescent` declines on the live run task and the graph is parked in `event.wait()`. It waited out its full 300s timeout and failed, while its pending request made every other snapshot operation answer `SAFE_POINT_BUSY` — and the gate is exactly where a user reaches for a restore. `wait_with_skip_time` now takes an optional `safe_point_hook` (the workflow's `await_restore_safe_point`), polled while blocked; time spent frozen is added back to the countdown so a restore hold cannot trip the auto-skip. Its five call sites cover the round, direction, integration, annotation, plan-review and species gates; the sixth gate is `_drive_hitl_gate` in `core.py`, the shared scaffold behind the LLM-failure, disabled-agent and marker-DB dialogs, which blocked on a bare `await event.wait()` with no hook *and no timeout* while `wait_with_skip_time` sat imported in the file and called nowhere in it. It now routes through the same helper, whose `wait_seconds=-1`-plus-hook branch preserves block-forever semantics exactly. Because the hook re-raises `WorkflowSupersededError` once a restore commits, that exception joins `asyncio.CancelledError` in the scaffold's cleanup branch so the gate does not leave `wait_input_reason` and its info attribute pinned on a state nobody will resume. A guard test fails if a gate is added without the hook.
  - **The headless runner is visible to the rest of the process (`backend/services/workflow_runner.py`)**: `start_workflow_if_needed` now registers its task in `_run_tasks` (and clears its own entry on exit). That dict is the process-wide proof that something is executing; the WebSocket runner was its only writer, so a v1-API run blocked at a HITL gate looked quiescent and a restore acknowledged its own safe point on behalf of a workflow that was very much alive and holding a live kernel — capturing pre_restore with `kernel_present: False` and tearing the checkpointer down under a running graph. The same handler now also clears the superseded flag, which every other `WorkflowSupersededError` site already did.
  - **Restore-job quiescence is re-proved, not proved once (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/jobs.py`)**: quiescence was established exactly once at enqueue (return value discarded), after which `_run_job` waited on nothing but `reservation.event`. That proof expires the moment the observed activity ends — and two of the five conditions `_acknowledge_if_quiescent` declines on, chat delegation and report generation, contain no acknowledgement site at all. A restore filed during either was orphaned deterministically: it burned its full 300s and reported `timed_out` on a session that had been idle for minutes, while its pending request made `_acquire_unlocked` refuse every other snapshot operation. The single `wait_for` is now a bounded poll against a monotonic deadline that re-runs `_acknowledge_if_quiescent` every `QUIESCENCE_RECHECK_SECONDS`; safe because the probe runs wholly under `state_lock` and the acknowledgement is generation-checked, so a run that registered meanwhile makes the probe decline and a workflow that acknowledged first leaves no pending request to match.
  - **A boundary capture that loses the safe point retries once (`backend/workflow/core.py`)**: the pending-request yield is a check-then-act whose only suspension point is the session lock a concurrent save holds across `to_thread`. A restore reserving the safe point in that window turned the required capture into a fail-closed gate that unwound the graph — so the workflow never reached another acknowledgement site and the restore that caused it waited out its own timeout. A *pending* request is a handover the workflow may complete, so it now does, once; an acquired foreign hold still fails closed.
  - **The post-report capture hands over a pending reservation too (`backend/workflow/reporting.py`)**: it was the one remaining required-snapshot producer that refused a merely *pending* safe point, and the report nodes contain no other acknowledgement site — so it is the last place a restore filed during report generation can be acknowledged. Both refusal routes ended the same way: without a live kernel the guard raised `LIVE_SANDBOX_REQUIRED` on `has_pending_request`, and on the live-kernel path the guard was skipped and the acquire below refused with `SAFE_POINT_BUSY`, since `_acquire_unlocked` cannot distinguish pending from acquired. Either armed the fail-closed gate on a run that had no snapshot problem, while the restore lost its only acknowledger. It now mirrors the boundary-capture handover, computing `kernel_present` *after* the freeze because a committed restore may have torn the sandbox down. An already-acquired foreign hold still fails closed — it never becomes pending.
  - **A parked session freezes its gate, but not a mid-node intervention (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/continuation.py`)**: `freeze_dynamic` short-circuited to `IdleCapsule` on `inactive: True`, and the quiescent-coordinator descriptor hard-codes that for every non-terminal status — including the `waiting*` statuses whose whole point is that the session is parked somewhere resumable. The status-derived gate lookup added in 0.36.8 was therefore unreachable for the producer it was written for: `pre_restore`, the snapshot a user reaches for to undo a restore, recorded no position at all. `inactive` still suppresses the mid-node `next_node` branch, where it genuinely contradicts a live graph position. Removing that short-circuit exposes a second question the status alone cannot answer: `waiting_input` also covers the `_drive_hitl_gate` interventions, and mapping those to `round_wait` froze a `SemanticGateCapsule` for a position the session was never at — restoring it set `wait_input_reason` to `round_wait` through the dispatcher's `or staged.gate` fallback while `agent_disabled_info` survived, so the reconnect resend no longer recognised the state and `/resume` — `round_wait` being a `SEMANTIC_WAIT_GATE` — answered `restored_wait_continuation` and drove the graph forward. `_gate_from_status` now returns None for `llm_failure`, `agent_disabled`, `marker_db_missing` and `snapshot_failure`, falling back to `IdleCapsule`: the honest answer, since the true position is mid-node and recovering it needs the checkpointer's `next`. The round gate, direction selection and integration selection are untouched — they all block in `_wait_for_user_input`.
  - **A restored gate lands on the status its own endpoint accepts (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/dispatcher.py`)**: the gate→status map is now a table. `plan_review` is a real capsule gate (`continuation.py` maps `waiting_plan_review` to it, and `freeze_dynamic` reaches that for `post_chat`/`pre_restore`), but every gate except `annotation_confirmation` was parked on `WAITING_INPUT`. `live_verify` only compares the gate, so the restore committed cleanly and left a session `POST /plan-review` rejects — with the workflow already evicted, its no-workflow branch, gated on exactly the status that was not written, was the only path left.
  - **A reused runtime does not drag the previous snapshot's position with it (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/pre_restore.py`)**: `capture_locked`'s runtime-reuse block re-pointed `analysis_state_ref`, `notebook_ref`, `checkpoint_ref` and `round_number` at the lineage head while the capsule built a few lines earlier was frozen from the LIVE session. The revision gate proves only that the kernel is unchanged, so this was two defects in one: unconditionally, the pre_restore snapshot re-declared the head's state, notebook and checkpoint, and a rollback to it silently discarded everything since that head which had not dirtied the kernel; and once the live round had advanced past the head, `manifest.round_number` was N while the capsule payload carried N+1, so `apply()` installed the round-N `session.json`, `activate()` overwrote `current_round` from the capsule, `live_verify` raised a `current_round` mismatch → `LIVE_VERIFY_FAILED`, and the session was left recovery-blocked. The reuse is now scoped to what the gate actually proves — `req.runtime = prev.runtime` plus `sandbox = None` — and the state/notebook/checkpoint are serialized fresh, which is close to free in a content-addressed store.
  - **Rollback is not silently half-undone, and reports the write it did make (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/recovery.py`, `backend/services/session_store.py`)**: `_persist_restored_state` installs the target state into the `sessions` cache before the transaction is verified, and recovery never touches that cache. So after a rollback rewrote `session.json` from the pre_restore snapshot, `ensure_session_loaded` returned the still-cached *target* object, cleared its recovery block and persisted it straight back over the file recovery had just restored — leaving an unblocked session whose state, files, notebook and checkpoint no longer agree. `RecoveryResult` now reports `session_state_rewritten` and the cache entry is evicted. That flag must also be reported when the rollback fails *after* its durable write: `rollback` streams the pre_restore state over the live `session.json` with the notebook and checkpoint steps still ahead of it, and when one of those failed the flag stayed False, the caller kept the cached target state, and `mark_recovery_blocked` wrote it straight back over the file the rollback had just restored — the same corruption by a different route. The restorer carries a `session_json_rewritten` flag, reset per transaction so it cannot leak across a scan, and both `except` handlers read it back. An extra eviction on a failure path is free; the next read comes from disk.
  - **A deferred recovery pass does not clear the block on the disk-load path (`backend/services/session_store.py`)**: of `ensure_session_loaded`'s three branches only the disk-load one omitted the `recovery_evaluated` term. Every lock-first endpoint (`/resume`, `/pause`, `/stop`, prompts, the interaction gates) takes the session lock before it loads, so `_snapshot_transaction_in_flight` — a bare probe of that same shared lock — always defers recovery for it; and once the cache-TTL sweeper has evicted a recovery-blocked session (it force-saves `recovery_blocked: True` and pops the cache but never touches `_recovery_blocked_sessions`), the deferred pass lands on exactly that branch, as does the eviction the rollback fix above performs. `clear_recovery_blocked` then discarded the process-wide marker, cleared the flag, stripped `recovery_error` and persisted the unblocked state, so `assert_recovery_clear` waved through a session whose `restore_apply` journal was still APPLYING and `/resume` built a workflow and started a kernel on a half-applied restore.
  - **Rollback quarantines a residual it never owned (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/recovery.py`, `backend/utils/runtime_apply.py`)**: with no `residual_ref` on the pre_restore manifest — the normal shape for a session with no live kernel, i.e. what users restore from — rollback skipped the residual block entirely and left the TARGET's pkl installed by `apply()`, with a valid signature. New `quarantine_live_runtime_pkl` mirrors the forward path's else-branch.
  - **`apply()` and rollback agree on who owns the checkpoint WAL (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/restore_parts/coordinator.py`, `recovery.py`)**: `apply` replaced only `checkpoints.sqlite`. Closing the checkpointer connection is what normally retires `-wal`/`-shm`, but that lives in `_retire_superseded_workflow`, which returns immediately when the session has no workflow object — the normal state after a restart, and one `_acknowledge_if_quiescent` supports — so orphans from an unclean shutdown were replayed over the restored image. Simply unlinking them is not the answer either: capture stores the checkpoint main file only, so commits still sitting in that WAL were never in any snapshot, and the WAL belongs to the very main file a rollback puts back — destroying it meant a restore that failed at a post-apply fence rolled back with those frames gone, while `rollback` returned True, `integrity_check` passed, nothing surfaced, and the session resumed at an earlier graph node. `apply` now moves the companions into `staging/<tx>/prev-checkpoints.sqlite-{wal,shm}` (falling back to an unlink if the move fails, since leaving them live is the corruption this addresses) and rollback reinstates them after restoring the main file. `forward_complete` never reads the stash — the pre-restore WAL must not be replayed over the target image.
  - **A crashed restore job stops protecting its target (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/recovery.py`)**: `_recover_restore_job_envelope` left every non-terminal `restore_job` journal in place, but `_run_job` is its only writer, so after a restart nothing could ever drive it terminal. `compute_protected_snapshot_ids` treats it as a live `restore_target`, so the snapshot became undeletable and un-reclaimable for good, status polls read `waiting_safe_point` forever, and `cancel` answered 404. A journal with no live handle is now marked `failed` with `RESTORE_JOB_ABANDONED`.
  - **A crashed export stops protecting its snapshots (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/exporter.py`, `recovery.py`)**: the same shape one journal kind over. `export()`'s own `finally` is the only remover and it runs in the process that wrote it, so a crash during the archive build — minutes over tens of GB, where a crash is most likely — left an ACTIVE journal with nothing to retire it, and `compute_protected_snapshot_ids` folds every `export_protection` envelope into the protected map: each snapshot the payload named answered 409 PROTECTED to DELETE and its bytes stayed unreclaimable by GC for the life of the session. "They only block deletes until an operator removes them" had no operator-facing removal path. Neither status nor age can tell debris from a live export — the journal reads ACTIVE for both, and the archive is built in a worker thread while recovery runs on the event loop, so recovery can legitimately land mid-export. The owner can: `_write_export_protection` now stamps `JOURNAL_OWNER_ID` as the import journal does, and `_recover_export_protection_envelope` reaps any journal carrying another process's id or none at all.
  - **The superseded flag is only set when something can clear it, and a superseded report run does not overwrite the restore (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/service.py`, `backend/workflow/core.py`, `backend/services/workflow_runner.py`)**: `_retire_superseded_workflow` marked the process-wide flag whenever a workflow *object* existed, but the object outlives its run and quiescent restores are admitted with nothing executing. Nothing then raises or catches `WorkflowSupersededError`, so the flag survived for the life of the process and a later, unrelated run unwound mid-round at its next node entry. The report paths get the same treatment from both ends. On the WebSocket path the flag *is* set and `generate_report`'s checkpointer-load `try` swallowed the exception from the node-entry hook, mislabelled it "Could not load state from checkpointer" and kept generating — producing reports and a post-report snapshot from the state the restore had discarded — while its trailing `except Exception` wrote FAILED, which `helpers.py` explicitly forbids for this exception; both handlers now delegate to `_superseded_report_result`, which also calls `clear_superseded()`, since no report path ever did. On the headless path the workflow is never registered in `workflows`, so `_retire_superseded_workflow` returns at its `workflow is None` guard and the flag is never set at all; `_build_workflow_for_session`'s `state_callback` had no is-current guard and wrote the pre-restore `AnalysisState` straight over the cache entry and `session.json` the restore had installed. That callback now compares object identity under `state_lock` — the headless counterpart of the WebSocket runner's `_write_state_if_current`, by identity because a committed restore installs a *new* state object and this workflow has no `workflows` entry to anchor on.
  - **The report runner closes the workflow it builds (`backend/services/workflow_runner.py`)**: `_run_report_generation` built a workflow and drove `generate_report`, which calls `initialize()` and therefore opens an aiosqlite WAL connection to the checkpoint database plus an `LLMManager`, a settings provider and the eager clients, and returned without `close()` — unlike the sibling `_headless_workflow_runner` whose `finally` does exactly that. The leak is bounded by cyclic GC, but the race is not incidental: the workflow is never in `workflows`, so `_retire_superseded_workflow` cannot reach it, and the orphaned connection can checkpoint stale WAL frames into a restored file.
  - **The three object writers are durable, proven and leak-free (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/object_store.py`)**: all three `put_*` writers fsynced the temp file and then `os.replace`d into `objects/` without fsyncing the directory, while `publish_staging` does fsync `committed/` — a power loss could leave a fully valid, HMAC-verified manifest whose objects were never linked, invisible to `verify_all_committed` (which checks signatures rather than existence), so the snapshot listed as healthy and only failed at restore. Cleanup was equally uneven: `put_file` had no `try`/`finally` at all and `put_stream`'s covered only its write loop, leaving `os.replace` guarded by nothing, so ENOSPC — the likeliest failure — stranded a near-complete multi-GB partial that nothing reclaims (recovery's staging sweep and `reconcile()` both skip dot-prefixed names by deliberate contract, quota accounting sums only `objects/`), and every HITL Retry leaked another copy. Both now have the same shape: one `try`, one `finally` that unlinks, a no-op once the rename has succeeded. `reconcile()` skips dot-prefixed entries too, so it is safe on its own terms rather than on its callers'. And `put_file` — the path for every h5ad, the dill residual, the checkpoint DB, the notebook and every workspace file — hashed the source in one pass and copied it in a second, unverified pass, making it the only writer with no post-write proof: a writer landing between the two passes got its bytes installed under the earlier hash, in a manifest that then commits and is signed clean, surfacing only at the restore rehash when the snapshot is already permanently unrestorable. It now accumulates a digest and byte count over the chunks it is already copying and refuses to install on a mismatch — no extra I/O.
  - **Both quota ceilings can actually fire (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/service_parts/capture_support.py`)**: the post-write gate `_check_quota` runs at capture step 8, after every object is on disk, yet subtracted `physical_added_bytes` from a `free` that already excluded them — demanding roughly twice the headroom the capture needed and failing deterministically on exactly the multi-GB AnnData captures this platform exists for, with Retry reproducing the same arithmetic forever. The pre-write estimate, for its part, summed only state, notebook, checkpoint and workspace — a few MB against the tens of GB a runtime capture writes — so the pre-I/O ceiling could essentially never fire for a runtime-bearing capture. It now projects the lineage head's runtime sizes, deduped against a local `seen` set only: deduping them against the store zeroes the projection for exactly the population it was written for, because GC marks every object of a verified committed manifest reachable and the head's own runtime objects are therefore present in `objects/` by construction. The head's refs model the bytes the next capture will write; they are not those bytes. Genuine reuse is short-circuited upstream, where `request.runtime is not None` returns 0 at the top.
